**Thailand**,[^cite_note-endo-10] officially the **Kingdom of Thailand**,[^cite_note-11] and formerly known as **Siam**,[^cite_note-siam-12] is a country located in [Mainland Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Southeast_Asia). It shares land borders with [Myanmar](19457.md) to the west and northwest, [Laos](17752.md) to the east and northeast, [Cambodia](334751.md) to the southeast, and [Malaysia](3607937.md) to the south. Its maritime boundaries include the [Gulf of Thailand](198107.md) and the [Andaman Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andaman_Sea), and it shares maritime borders with [Vietnam](202354.md), [Indonesia](14579.md), and [India](14533.md). Thailand has a population of nearly 66 million people[^cite_note-13] and covers an area of approximately 513,115 km<sup>2</sup> (198,115 sq mi).[^cite_note-14] The [capital](181337.md) and [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_municipalities_in_Thailand#Largest_cities_by_urban_population) is [Bangkok](56615.md).[^cite_note-CapitalCity-15]

Archaeological evidence indicates that humans have inhabited the area for at least 40,000 years. Indigenous ethnic groups include the [Mon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mon_people), [Khmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_people), and [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_people) people. The [Tai people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tai_people) are believed to have originated from the [Điện Biên Phủ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C4%90i%E1%BB%87n_Bi%C3%AAn_Ph%E1%BB%A7) region since the 5th century and began migrating into the territory of modern Thailand between the 8th and 10th centuries. During the classical historical period, major kingdoms such as [Sukhothai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_Kingdom), [Lan Na](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lan_Na), and [Ayutthaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Kingdom) were established. The Sukhothai Kingdom is regarded as the beginning of [Thai history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_history), while the Ayutthaya Kingdom, founded in 1350 CE, became a [regional power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power) replacing the [Khmer Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Empire). European contact began in 1511 when [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire) envoys arrived in Ayutthaya. The Ayutthaya Kingdom flourished until its complete destruction during the [1765–1767 Burmese–Siamese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese%E2%80%93Siamese_War_\(1765%E2%80%931767\)) by the [Burmese forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Burmese_Armed_Forces) under the [Konbaung dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konbaung_dynasty) in 1767.

After the fall of Ayutthaya, [King Taksin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Taksin) reunified the kingdom and established the [Thonburi Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thonburi_Kingdom), which lasted only 15 years before he was overthrown by [Phutthayotfa Chulalok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rama_I) (Rama I), the founder of the [Chakri dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chakri_dynasty). King Rama I established the [Rattanakosin Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rattanakosin_Kingdom) and moved the capital to Bangkok in 1782. During the era of [Western imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_imperialism_in_Asia), Siam remained the only country in [Southeast Asia](28741.md) to avoid colonisation by European powers, although it ceded territory, trade rights, and legal privileges through several [unequal treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unequal_treaties).[^cite_note-16] The governance system evolved into an [absolute monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy) centralised under the rule of [King Chulalongkorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Chulalongkorn) (Rama V). Siam [adapted to international relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1897_visit_by_Chulalongkorn_to_Europe) during the imperialist era and joined [World War I](4764461.md) on the side of the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I), a political decision aimed at revising the effects of unequal treaties and enhancing Siam's international status.

Following the [Siamese revolution of 1932](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siamese_revolution_of_1932) by the [Khana Ratsadon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khana_Ratsadon) (lit. 'People's Party'), Siam transitioned to a [parliamentary constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_constitutional_monarchy) and was officially renamed Thailand. During [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_in_Thailand), the country was under the [military dictatorship](146728.md) of [Plaek Phibunsongkhram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaek_Phibunsongkhram) and allied with the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan) as part of the [Axis powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers), but Thailand did not become a defeated nation due to the underground [Free Thai Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Thai_Movement)'s "Declaration of Peace", which was recognised by the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_in_World_War_II). During the [Cold War](325329.md), Thailand became a key [major non-NATO ally](3812445.md) of the [United States](3434750.md) and played a major role in [countering communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism) in the [Korean War](16772.md), the [Vietnam War](32611.md), and other [proxy wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxy_wars). Thailand also joined the [Southeast Asia Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia_Treaty_Organization) (SEATO). In the 1970s through 1990s, Thailand alternated between [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy) and military dictatorship.

In the 21st century, Thailand has experienced political conflict between supporters and opponents of Prime Minister [Thaksin Shinawatra](360815.md), leading to the [2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Thai_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) and [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Thai_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) coups d'état. Thailand operates under the [2017 Constitution](6840408.md) and a [coalition government](6036.md) following the [2019 Thai general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Thai_general_election), as well as [political demonstrations demanding democracy and monarchy reform](64637075.md). The current constitutional structure allows *de facto* political influence by the [Royal Thai Armed Forces](30136.md).[^cite_note-Abuza2021-17][^cite_note-18][^cite_note-19] Thailand has faced border tensions from the [2008–2011 Cambodian–Thai border crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008%E2%80%932011_Cambodian%E2%80%93Thai_border_crisis) extending to the [2025 Cambodian–Thai border crisis](80127921.md) involving territorial disputes and military clashes.

Thailand is recognised as a [developing country](78449.md) and holds significant geopolitical importance as a [regional power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power) in Southeast Asia. A [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) governed under a [parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system), Thailand promotes its shared values and interests through participation in international organisations including the [United Nations](31769.md) and the [Association of Southeast Asian Nations](106539.md) (ASEAN), and as a [major non-NATO ally](3812445.md) of the United States. As a [newly industrialised country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newly_industrialized_country), its advanced [economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Thailand) ranks among the largest in the region, relying chiefly upon manufacturing, agriculture, and [tourism](6188546.md).[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21]

## Etymology

Before 1939, Thailand[^cite_note-endo-10] was known internationally as *Siam*.[^cite_note-siam-12] The word *Thai* (ไทย)[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] is traditionally interpreted to mean 'free man' in the [Thai language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language), distinguishing the Thai from [serfs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serfdom) in historical society.[^cite_note-Coedes_1968-24]<sup>: 197 </sup> However, other scholars suggest the term originally meant simply 'people' or 'human being', noting its historical use in rural areas.[^cite_note-Phumisak_1992-25]

Thais typically refer to their country using the polite form *prathet Thai* ([Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language): ประเทศไทย) or the more colloquial *mueang Thai* ([Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language): เมืองไทย). The word *[mueang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueang)*, which archaically referred to a [city-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandala_\(political_model\)), is commonly used to denote a city or region. The country's official name, *Ratcha-anachak Thai* ([Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language): ราชอาณาจักรไทย), translates to 'Kingdom of Thailand'. Etymologically, its components derive from [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) and [Pali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pali): *ratcha* ('royal'), *ana* ('authority'), and *-chak* ('wheel', a symbol of power).

The former name *Siam* may have originated from the Sanskrit word *śyāma* (श्याम, 'dark')[^cite_note-Phumisak_1992-25] or the [Mon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mon_language) word *rhmañña* ('stranger'), which likely shares a root with *[Shan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shan_people)* and *[Assam](186162.md)*.[^cite_note-26] Another theory suggests the name derives from the Chinese term *Xiān*, used to refer to the region. Following the merger of local kingdoms in 1349, Chinese records began referring to the [Ayutthaya Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Kingdom) as *Xiānluó* (暹羅).[^cite_note-xian-27]<sup>: 40 </sup>[^cite_note-29] The ancient Khmers also used *Siam* to refer to the people settled in the [Chao Phraya River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chao_Phraya_River) valley, as recorded in 13th-century inscriptions.[^cite_note-sac-30]

Ornate handwritten Latin signature reading SPPM Mongkut Rex Siamensium.

*SPPM Mongkut Rex Siamensium*, [King Mongkut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongkut)'s signature

The name *Siam* gained official status in international relations during the 19th century, notably appearing in the [Bowring Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowring_Treaty) under the signature of King [Mongkut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongkut) (r. 1851–1868), who signed as *Mongkut Rex Siamensium* (Mongkut, King of the Siamese). Following the transition to a constitutional monarchy, the country was officially renamed *Thailand* on 24 June 1939.[^cite_note-31]

## History

Main article: [History of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Thailand)

### Prehistory and origins

Main article: [Prehistoric Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Thailand)

Continuous human habitation in present-day Thailand dates back at least 20,000 years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELePoer19894-32] Agriculture emerged with [rice cultivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_rice_cultivation) around 2000 BCE,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit20174-33] alongside participation in the Maritime Jade Road, a trading network connecting Southeast Asia from 2000 BCE to 1000 CE.[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37] The [Ban Chiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ban_Chiang) site in northeast Thailand is the earliest known centre of copper and bronze production in [Southeast Asia](28741.md), dating between 2000 and 1000 BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit20174-33][^cite_note-38] Iron working appeared around 500 BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit20175-39]

The Indochinese Peninsula was heavily influenced by the [culture and religions of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India) from the time of [Funan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funan), established in the 2nd century BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELePoer19895-40][^cite_note-41] By the 6th century, early multiethnic kingdoms emerged, including the [Mon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mon_people) principalities of [Dvaravati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dvaravati) and [Hariphunchai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hariphunchai).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt20015-42] In the 9th century, the [Khmer people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_people) established the [Khmer Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_Empire), centred in [Angkor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angkor), while [Tambralinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tambralinga), a Malay state, rose to control trade through the [Strait of Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Malacca) in the 10th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELePoer19895,_7-43]

The [Thai people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_people) belong to the [Tai ethnolinguistic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tai_peoples). While their exact origins are debated, historians suggest their ancestors migrated from southern China and northern Vietnam into present-day Thailand between the 6th and 11th centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt19842,_6-44] Migrating into territory occupied by Mon and Khmer populations, the Tai intermixed with local groups and absorbed Indian, Mon, and Khmer cultural influences.[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKeyes1997203-46] Modern studies indicate that "Thai" serves more as a cultural designation resulting from this integration rather than a distinct genetic ethnicity.[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-genetic-48][^cite_note-49]

The term *Syam* first appears in the 11th and 12th centuries in [Champa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champa) epigraphy and the [bas-reliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bas-relief) of [Angkor Wat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angkor_Wat), which depict warriors and prisoners of war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoedès1968190–191,_194–195-50] Pre-14th-century [Mainland Southeast Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_Southeast_Asia) sources primarily used *Syam* as an [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym) for a cultural group distinct from the Khmer, Cham, Mon, or Bagan. Chinese sources used *Xian* as a [toponym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toponym) for the region.[^cite_note-Smith_2019-51][^cite_note-52]

### Early Tai confederate cities (673 BCE – 13th century CE)

Main article: [Initial states of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initial_states_of_Thailand)

Map showing Kra–Dai language distribution in East and Southeast Asia with arrows marking Tai migration routes southward from China.

Map showing geographic distribution of [Kra–Dai linguistic family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kra%E2%80%93Dai_languages). Arrows represent general pattern of the migration of [Tai-speaking tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tai_peoples) along the rivers and over the lower passes.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201727-53]

Map highlighting the geographic territory of the Lan Na kingdom in northern Thailand.

[Lan Na](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lan_Na) kingdom during the reign of [Tilokaraj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tilokaraj) (r. 1441–1487)

Ancestors of the [Tai-speaking peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tai_peoples) are believed to have originated in the [Yangtze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yangtze) basin as early as the 12th century BCE. Following conflicts with Chinese expansion in [Guangxi](47836950.md), Tai groups gradually migrated southwestwards along rivers and mountain passes into Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-Chamberlain_2016-54][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201726-55] By the first millennium CE, they had established early city-states across present-day northern Thailand, Laos, and northeastern Myanmar, becoming a dominant regional power.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJumsai1967-56]

According to northern chronicles, early Tai confederacies like [Singhanavati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singhanavati) and its successor [Ngoenyang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngoenyang) were established in northern Thailand during the first millennium CE.[^cite_note-wieng-57]<sup>: 5, 9 </sup>[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit20148-28] In 1262, King [Mangrai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrai) relocated the Ngoenyang capital to [Chiang Mai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Mai), founding the [Lan Na](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lan_Na) kingdom. Mangrai unified surrounding principalities and expanded southwards, annexing the Mon kingdom of [Hariphunchai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hariphunchai) in 1292.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit20148-28][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoedès1968208-58]

Further south, Tai migrants interacted with indigenous [Khmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_people) and [Mon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mon_people) populations through conflict and royal intermarriage.[^cite_note-krai-59][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThepthani195346–49,_83–86-60] By the 12th century, Tai leaders controlled cities in the upper Chao Phraya basin. Their gradual displacement of Khmer and Mon influence culminated in the establishment of the [Sukhothai Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_Kingdom) in 1238, marking a major shift in the region's political landscape.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt198452–53-61]

### Dvaravati (7th–11th centuries)

Main article: [Dvaravati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dvaravati)

Map depicting the positions of moated settlement sites of the Dvaravati culture across modern Thailand.

Map of settlements of Dvaravati culture from the 6th to 9th centuries

Black and white photograph showing the ruined brick base of a Dvaravati-style chedi.

Dvaravati-style Chedi at [Wat Phra Rub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat_Phra_Rub?action=edit\&redlink=1), [Suphan Buri province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suphan_Buri_province), now completely destroyed.

The Chao Phraya River was home to the Mon Dvaravati culture, which flourished from the 7th to the 11th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt200318-62] Samuel Beal identified the polity in Chinese records as "Duoluobodi". Early 20th-century excavations established [Nakhon Pathom Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakhon_Pathom_Province) and U Thong as major centres. Dvaravati inscriptions used [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) and [Mon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mon_language) written in scripts derived from the [Pallava alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallava_alphabet).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201711-63]

Dvaravati is associated with [Theravada Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada), likely transmitted from Sri Lanka, while its ruling class participated in [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) rites. [Dvaravati art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dvaravati_art) shows strong Indian influence, whereas the eastern Chao Phraya valley displays greater Khmer and Hindu influence. Dvaravati culture spread into [Isan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isan) and southwards to the [Kra Isthmus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kra_Isthmus). It operated as a network of city-states linked through the [mandala political model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandala_\(political_model\)). Baker and Pasuk challenge the idea that the plain was incorporated into a unified Lavo-Khmer polity, citing the absence of Angkorian temple-endowment inscriptions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201722,_24-64]

The name *Dvaravati* derives from medallions bearing the Sanskrit phrase *śrīdvāravatīśvarapuṇya* found at Nakhon Pathom and other sites. Chinese and Annamese records mention *To-lo-po-ti*, with tribute missions reaching the [Tang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tang_dynasty) court in 638, 640 and 649.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201716,_18-65]

Art historians later extended the term *Dvaravati* to describe a wider body of styles and their corresponding period. Baker and Pasuk argue these styles spread through trade rather than political authority. Between the 6th and 12th centuries, forty moated towns shared religious practices, artistic traditions and Mon inscriptions but showed little political integration.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201718,_21,_24-66]

Recent research places the coastline near its present position by about 500 CE, challenging older views of a submerged lower gulf. The religious landscape was also diverse; images of [Avalokiteśvara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avalokite%C5%9Bvara) and [Prajñāpāramitā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praj%C3%B1%C4%81p%C4%81ramit%C4%81) appeared alongside Buddha images, reflecting mixed Buddhist practices rather than strict divisions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit20173,_13,_15-67]

#### Lavo

Main article: [Lavo Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavo_Kingdom)

Three large stone Khmer-style prang towers stand in a line beneath the sky.

Wat [Phra Prang Sam Yod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phra_Prang_Sam_Yod), a 13th-century Angkorian temple in [Lopburi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lopburi).

Lavo belonged to the Dvaravati sphere early in the period. Chronicle traditions claim Lavapura was founded in 648—or possibly 538—by [Kalavarnadisharaja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalavarnadisharaja).[^cite_note-nan-68]<sup>: 34 </sup> The only attested native language is [Old Mon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Mon_language).[^cite_note-ori-69] Baker and Pasuk identify [Lopburi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lopburi) as Lavapura.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201716-70]

Around the 10th century, central plain city-states reorganised into two mandalas: Lavo in the east and [Suvarnabhumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suvarnabhumi) (modern [Suphan Buri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suphan_Buri)) in the west.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThepthani1953-71] According to Northern Chronicles, a king of [Tambralinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tambralinga) installed a Malay prince in Lavo in 903 or 927.[^cite_note-monwar-72] This prince married a Khmer princess, and their son became [Suryavarman I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suryavarman_I), linking Lavo to Khmer domination. However, Baker and Pasuk argue Angkorian control of Lavo was asserted rather than demonstrated, and the relationship remained flexible.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201724-73]

Angkor lost Lavo to [Suphannabhum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suphannabhum) princes in 1052, moving the seat to [Ayodhya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phra_Nakhon_Si_Ayutthaya_\(city\)) under [Narai I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narai_I). Angkor pushed to recover influence in 1181 when [Jayavarman VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jayavarman_VII) appointed a kinsman to govern Lavapura.[^cite_note-indra-74] This pressured Lavo ruler [Sri Dharmasokaraja II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Dharmasokaraja_II) to flee to Nakhon Si Thammarat.[^cite_note-ori-69]<sup>: 38–39 </sup> Lavapura was likely recovered in the 13th century by a Tai prince.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThepthani1953109-75] Angkorian power eventually waned,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHigham2014120-76] and in 1238, [Bang Klang Hao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Si_Inthrathit) took Sukhothai from [Khom Sabat Khlon Lamphong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khom_Sabat_Khlon_Lamphong), conventionally marking the kingdom's foundation.[^cite_note-lost-77][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoedès1968195–196-78]

#### Siam people

Early references to the Siam people occur in pre-Angkorian inscriptions. A 661 inscription from [Angkor Borei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angkor_Borei) mentions a female slave named *Ku Sayam*, while a 682 inscription records a noble named Sayam.[^cite_note-eth-79] Tatsuo Hoshino identifies several polities in Tang annals as early Tai or Siamese states linking [Champa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champa) with the Chao Phraya basin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoshino200239,_53–54,_68-80] [Hiram Woodward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiram_Woodward) considers this linguistic argument tendentious.[^cite_note-woodward-81]<sup>: 87 </sup>

Song period Chinese sources describe *Xiān guó* (暹國) in the western [Chao Phraya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chao_Phraya) region, while the east was associated with [Lavo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavo_Kingdom).[^cite_note-ngr-82] By the 13th and 14th centuries, Siamese polities like [Phrip Phri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phrip_Phri), [Suphan Buri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suphan_Buri), [Ayodhya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Kingdom) and [Sukhothai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_Kingdom) appeared independently in Chinese records.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201745,_51,_55-83] Evidence for relations with Chenla appears in the Wat Kud Tae inscription, recording a royal marriage.[^cite_note-ally-84] A [Champa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champa) inscription records Siamese and Angkorian forces attacking [Vijaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vijaya_\(Champa\)) in 1201.[^cite_note-c3-85] [Zhou Daguan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhou_Daguan) reported Siamese influence over Lavapura and their presence in [Yaśodharapura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ya%C5%9Bodharapura).[^cite_note-Reviewed_Work:_A_record_of_Cambodia-86][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZhou2007-87]

### Xiān and Chen Li Fu (12th–14th centuries)

Main articles: [Xiān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi%C4%81n) and [Chen Li Fu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Li_Fu)

Map showing the geographic scope and proposed boundary of the Phrip Phri polity.

Proposed extent of the Xiān polity of [Phrip Phri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phrip_Phri) in the late 12th century.

Before Xiān, Chinese records describe [Chên Li Fu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Li_Fu), a polity west of [Chenla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chenla).[^cite_note-walailak-88]<sup>: 7–11 </sup> It sent embassies to the [Song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_dynasty) court in the early 1200s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt200120-89] Its ruler, Sri Mahidharavarman, bore the Khmer title *kamrateng*. Its tribute included elephants, ivory, and rhinoceros horn,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201748-90] but the Song court instructed Chên Li Fu to send no further tribute due to distance.[^cite_note-wolters-91]<sup>: 6 </sup>

The earliest notices of Xiān are Vietnamese, detailing merchant ships from Xiān reaching *Hǎidōng* (海東) in 1149.[^cite_note-ctext-92]<sup>: line 61 </sup> Xiān sent tribute in 1182,[^cite_note-ctext-92]<sup>: line 25 </sup> and merchants returned in 1241.[^cite_note-ctext-92]<sup>: line 29 </sup> Chinese records use [Xiān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi%C4%81n) for polities of the lower [Chao Phraya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chao_Phraya_River), which [Yoneo Ishii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoneo_Ishii) interpreted as several port polities rather than a single state.[^cite_note-xian-27]<sup>: 38, 41–2 </sup> Xiān first appears in the Chinese record in 1282 or 1283 and sent tribute missions frequently between 1292 and 1323. [Lavo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavo_Kingdom), [Suphan Buri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suphan_Buri), [Ayodhya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Kingdom) and Phetchaburi each dealt with China in its own name before the union of 1349.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201745,_51,_55-83]

The [Yuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_dynasty) court's dealings with Xiān indicate a negotiated relationship. Envoys sent to demand submission in 1293 were refused, and the king came in person with tribute in 1295.[^cite_note-xian-27]<sup>: 38–9 </sup> An edict of 1295 ordered Xiān not to harm *Ma-li-yu-er* ([Melayu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melayu_Kingdom)), with whom it had long been at war.[^cite_note-supakarn-93]<sup>: 67 </sup> The older identification of Xiān with [Sukhothai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_Kingdom) was challenged in 1989 by [Yamamoto Tatsurō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamamoto_Tatsur%C5%8D), who noted that a gazetteer places Sukhothai under Xiān and therefore distinguishes the two.[^cite_note-xian-27]<sup>: 37, 38 </sup>

### Sukhothai Kingdom (1238–1438)

Main article: [Sukhothai Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_Kingdom)

[Sukhothai Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_Kingdom)

Map of Southeast Asia in the late 13th century showing Sukhothai and neighboring realms

Sukhothai and neighbours, end of 13th century CE

Large seated stone Buddha image visible through a tall open doorway at Wat Si Chum

Phra Achana, Wat Si Chum, Sukhothai Historical Park

Ancient stone chedis and pillars of Wat Mahathat reflected in a tranquil lake

The ruins of Wat Mahathat, [Sukhothai Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_Historical_Park)

After the decline of the Khmer Empire and [Kingdom of Pagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagan_Kingdom) in the early 13th century, Tai domains stretched from present-day India to the [Malay Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Peninsula).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt198438–39-94] During the 13th century, Tai people settled in the core lands of Dvaravati and Lavo down to [Nakhon Si Thammarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakhon_Si_Thammarat_Kingdom).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt198450–51-95]

In 1238, [Pho Khun Bang Klang Hao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Si_Inthrathit) rallied local populations to rebel against the Khmer, crowning himself the first king of the [Sukhothai Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_Kingdom).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt198452–53-61] Thai historians count Sukhothai as the first kingdom of the Thai people. It expanded furthest under [Ram Khamhaeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram_Khamhaeng), functioning mostly as a network of local lords rather than a tightly controlled empire.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt198455–56-96] He is believed to have invented the [Thai script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_script). Sukhothai embraced [Theravada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada) [Buddhism](3267529.md) under [Maha Thammaracha I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maha_Thammaracha_I).

### Ayutthaya Kingdom (1351–1767)

Main articles: [Ayutthaya Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Kingdom), [Galeote Pereira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galeote_Pereira), [Burmese–Siamese War (1547–1549)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese%E2%80%93Siamese_War_\(1547%E2%80%931549\)), and [Burmese–Siamese War (1584–1593)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese%E2%80%93Siamese_War_\(1584%E2%80%931593\))

[Ayutthaya Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayutthaya_Kingdom)

Map depicting the territorial domain of the Ayutthaya Kingdom around 1390 CE

Ayutthaya and neighbours, c. 1390 CE

Map depicting the territorial expanse of the Ayutthaya Kingdom around 1545 CE

Ayutthaya and neighbours, c. 1540 CE

17th-century Dutch painting showing a bird's-eye view of the fortified island city of Ayutthaya

Painting of Ayutthaya city c. 1665, by [Johannes Vingboons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Vingboons)

The Ayutthaya Kingdom rose from the earlier [Lavo Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavo_Kingdom) and [Suphannaphum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suphannaphum) with [Uthong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uthong) as its first king. Ayutthaya was a patchwork of self-governing principalities and tributary provinces under the [mandala system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandala_\(political_model\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHigham1989355-97] Its initial expansion was achieved through conquest and political marriage.

Before the end of the 15th century, Ayutthaya invaded the Khmer Empire three times, sacking its capital [Angkor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angkor).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKasetsiri200526-98] This marked a major geopolitical shift, allowing Ayutthaya to eclipse the Khmer Empire in mainland Southeast Asia. Sukhothai was steadily incorporated into the kingdom through dynastic marriages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201759–61-99] King [Borommatrailokkanat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borommatrailokkanat) brought about bureaucratic reforms and created the *[sakdina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakdina)* social hierarchy, instituting male [corvée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corv%C3%A9e) labour.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013107-100]

European contact began with the [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_people) in 1511.[^cite_note-101] The French, Dutch, and English followed in the 17th century. Rivalry for supremacy over Chiang Mai pitted Ayutthaya against Burma's [Taungoo dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taungoo_dynasty), leading to the [capture of the capital in 1570](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese%E2%80%93Siamese_War_\(1568%E2%80%9370\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013146–147-102] This brief period of vassalage ended when [Naresuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naresuan) proclaimed independence in 1584.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit201411-103]

The kingdom prospered during [Narai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narai)'s reign (1656–1688), becoming an Asian great power alongside China and India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBakerPhongpaichit2017ix-104] Growing French influence, however, led to nationalist backlash and the [Siamese revolution of 1688](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siamese_revolution_of_1688).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013185–186-105] After dynastic struggles, Ayutthaya entered a "[golden age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_age)" of flourishing [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_art) and [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_literature). The final fifty years were marked by violent succession crises. In 1765, the Burmese under the [Alaungpaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaungpaya) dynasty [invaded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese%E2%80%93Siamese_War_\(1759%E2%80%9360\)), and after a 14-month siege, Ayutthaya fell and was burned in April 1767.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarvey1925250-106][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERuangsilp2007218-107]

### Thonburi Kingdom (1767–1782)

Main article: [Thonburi Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thonburi_Kingdom)

Following Ayutthaya's fall, the region fractured into five warlord domains. [Chao Tak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taksin), a military leader based in [Chanthaburi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chanthaburi), successfully retook Ayutthaya from the Burmese just seven months later.[^cite_note-108]

Crowning himself King [Taksin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taksin), he established [Thonburi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thonburi) as his temporary capital. He subdued rival warlords and waged wars to [drive the Burmese out of Lan Na](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese%E2%80%93Siamese_War_\(1774%E2%80%931775\)) in 1775,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013225-109] [capture Vientiane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lao%E2%80%93Siamese_War_\(1778%E2%80%931779\)) in 1778,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013227–228-110] and [install a pro-Thai king in Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siamese%E2%80%93Vietnamese_War_\(1769%E2%80%931773\)). In his final years, his alleged insanity led to a coup, resulting in Taksin's execution by General [Chao Phraya Chakri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rama_I). Chakri became the first king of the [Chakri dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chakri_dynasty) and founded the [Rattanakosin Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rattanakosin_Kingdom) on 6 April 1782.[^cite_note-111]

### Rattanakosin Kingdom and modernisation (1782–1932)

Main article: [Rattanakosin Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rattanakosin_Kingdom_\(1782%E2%80%931932\))

[Rattanakosin Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rattanakosin_Kingdom)

Detailed map of Siam in 1900 showing internal administrative boundaries, monthon, and provinces

Detailed map of Siam's provinces, vassals, and monthons in 1900

Emerald Buddha statue adorned in gold garments seated high atop an ornate golden altar in Wat Phra Kaew

[Emerald Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerald_Buddha) in [Wat Phra Kaew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat_Phra_Kaew). Considered the sacred palladium of Thailand.

Historic photograph of King Chulalongkorn seated next to Tsar Nicholas II in 1897

[Chulalongkorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chulalongkorn) with [Nicholas II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_II) in Saint Petersburg, 1897

Map displaying the maximal extent of Siamese territorial dominion and vassal states in 1805

Siamese territory and dominion at its largest in 1805, resulting from the [Burmese–Siamese War (1802–1805)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese%E2%80%93Siamese_War_\(1802%E2%80%931805\))

Under Rama I, Rattanakosin defended against Burmese incursions and established suzerainty over large parts of Laos and Cambodia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENolan20021653-112] In 1821, [John Crawfurd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Crawfurd) negotiated trade with Siam.[^cite_note-113] Bangkok signed the [Burney Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burney_Treaty) in 1826.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013281-114]

[Anouvong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anouvong)'s [Lao rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lao_rebellion_\(1826%E2%80%931828\)) was suppressed in 1826, destroying Vientiane.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013283–286-115] Bangkok also fought [several wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siamese%E2%80%93Vietnamese_wars) with Vietnam to regain Cambodia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013290–292-116]

Siam faced pressure from Western powers. King [Mongkut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongkut) (1851–1868) signed the [Bowring Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bowring_Treaty), the first of many [unequal treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unequal_treaty), which nevertheless spurred trade and development.[^cite_note-117] King [Chulalongkorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chulalongkorn) accelerated modernisation, abolishing slavery and the corvée system, and centralising administration into a modern nation-state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013327,_347,_362–363-118] The [1893 Franco-Siamese crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1893_Franco-Siamese_crisis) cost Siam territories east of the Mekong.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013350–353-119]

Thailand remains the only Southeast Asian state never colonised by a Western power,[^cite_note-120] partly because Britain and France maintained it as a [buffer state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buffer_state).[^cite_note-121] Under [Vajiravudh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajiravudh), nationalist ideas spread,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013404-122] and Siam [joined the First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siam_in_World_War_I) on the side of [the Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I), allowing it to eventually revoke extraterritoriality.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013407–408-123]

### Military dictatorship (1932–1973)

Main articles: [Thailand in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thailand_in_World_War_II) and [History of Thailand (1932–1973)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Thailand_\(1932%E2%80%931973\))

Formal portrait of Field Marshal Plaek Phibunsongkhram in decorated military uniform.

Field Marshal Plaek Phibunsongkhram

Economic hardships during the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) undermined the absolute monarchy. The bloodless [Siamese revolution of 1932](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siamese_revolution_of_1932) led by the [Khana Ratsadon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khana_Ratsadon) forced King [Prajadhipok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prajadhipok) to sign a constitution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELePoer198925-124] Following rebellions, military influence grew. King Prajadhipok abdicated, and the young [Ananda Mahidol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ananda_Mahidol) became king.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013448–449-125]

Military strongman [Plaek Phibunsongkhram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaek_Phibunsongkhram) became premier in 1938, launching a nationalist, [Irredentist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irredentism), and pro-fascist agenda, changing the country's name to "Thailand".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELePoer198928-126] In 1940, Phibun launched the [Franco-Thai War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Thai_War) to reclaim territories from [French Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Indochina).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013462-127] Following a brief resistance against the [Japanese invasion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_invasion_of_Thailand) in 1941, Thailand allied with Japan and declared war on the US and UK.[^cite_note-128] The [Free Thai Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Thai_Movement) resisted the occupation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013465–466-129]

King Bhumibol Adulyadej standing at a podium addressing a joint session of Congress.

King [Bhumibol Adulyadej](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhumibol_Adulyadej) addresses a [joint session of the United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_session_of_the_United_States_Congress), 1960.

In 1946, King Ananda died under mysterious circumstances, and [Bhumibol Adulyadej](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhumibol_Adulyadej) ascended to the throne. Phibun returned via coup in 1947, aligned Thailand with the US in the [Korean War](16772.md), and helped establish the [Southeast Asia Treaty Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia_Treaty_Organization) (SEATO).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013493-130] He was ousted in 1957 by Field Marshal [Sarit Thanarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarit_Thanarat), who reinstated autocracy, rooted legitimacy in the monarchy, and aligned with the US during the [Vietnam War](32611.md), executing an [anti-communist campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communist_mass_killings#Thailand).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013523-131] The era saw rapid modernisation and urbanisation, but bred opposition among a growing middle class and students.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013534-132]

### Transition towards democracy (1973–2001)

Main article: [History of Thailand (1973–2001)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Thailand_\(1973%E2%80%932001\))

The [1973 Thai popular uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Thai_popular_uprising) overthrew the military regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013541–543-133] King Bhumibol appointed a civilian prime minister, initiating a brief parliamentary democracy.[^cite_note-Britannica2-134] Political instability and fears of communist expansion culminated in the [Thammasat University massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6_October_1976_massacre) and a return to right-wing military rule.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWyatt2013548–549-135]

Under [Prem Tinsulanonda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prem_Tinsulanonda) in the 1980s, Thailand enjoyed economic growth and semi-democracy ("premocracy"), successfully ending the communist insurgency.[^cite_note-136] In 1988, [Chatichai Choonhavan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatichai_Choonhavan) became the first freely elected prime minister since 1976. A 1991 coup and subsequent attempt by [Suchinda Kraprayoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suchinda_Kraprayoon) to retain power sparked the [Black May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_May_\(Thailand\)) protests in 1992, resulting in a bloody crackdown until King Bhumibol intervened.[^cite_note-137] Free elections returned, leading to the creation of the progressive [1997 "People's Constitution."](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_constitution_of_Thailand)

### Shinawatra era (2001–present)

Main article: [History of Thailand (2001–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Thailand_\(2001%E2%80%93present\))

The [1997 Asian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Asian_financial_crisis) devastated the economy, necessitating an [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) bailout.[^cite_note-138] The crisis propelled populist [Thaksin Shinawatra](360815.md) and his [Thai Rak Thai Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_Rak_Thai_Party) to power. Thaksin's policies reduced rural poverty and provided universal healthcare,[^cite_note-139] but alienated the urban elite and royalists. Crises including the [southern Thailand insurgency](2326869.md) and the [2004 Indian Ocean earthquake and tsunami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Indian_Ocean_earthquake_and_tsunami) further polarised the nation.[^cite_note-140]

Massive protests by the conservative and royalist [People's Alliance for Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Alliance_for_Democracy) (PAD) led to a [military coup in 2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Thai_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) that ousted Thaksin.[^cite_note-Phongpaichit-2008-141]

Large crowd of protesters clad in red shirts gathering on a Bangkok city street.

[United Front for Democracy Against Dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Front_for_Democracy_Against_Dictatorship), Red Shirts, [protest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Thai_political_protests) in 2010

Following the coup, resistance persisted. The Thaksin-aligned [People's Power Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Power_Party_\(Thailand\)) won elections in 2007, but [PAD protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Thai_political_crisis) led to its dissolution, installing the Democrat Party. Pro-Thaksin forces under the [United Front for Democracy Against Dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Front_for_Democracy_Against_Dictatorship) (UDD) intensely protested both [in 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Thai_political_unrest) and [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Thai_political_protests).

Following a period of intense political polarisation, the military seized power in a [2014 coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Thai_coup_d'%C3%A9tat) led by General [Prayut Chan-o-cha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prayut_Chan-o-cha), establishing a junta that governed for nearly a decade.[^cite_note-142] The [2023 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Thai_general_election) marked a significant transition of power away from military-aligned parties to a coalition led by the [Pheu Thai Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pheu_Thai_Party), ending nine years of royalist-military rule.[^cite_note-143]

The subsequent years were characterised by renewed political instability and significant judicial intervention, which led to the consecutive removals of prime ministers [Srettha Thavisin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srettha_Thavisin) and [Paetongtarn Shinawatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paetongtarn_Shinawatra).[^cite_note-144] Amidst this domestic turbulence, temporary [violent clashes along the Cambodian border](80127921.md) in 2025 shifted national focus.[^cite_note-145] The instability culminated in the parliamentary election of [Anutin Charnvirakul](60867789.md) as prime minister, who quickly dissolved the House and led his [Bhumjaithai Party](20737916.md) to victory in the [2026 election](74706351.md), marking a major electoral revival for Thai conservatism.[^cite_note-146]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Thailand)

Rocky tropical coastline with ancient rock strata and calm clear waters.

[Ko Tarutao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ko_Tarutao), [Satun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satun_Province)

Totalling 513,120 square kilometres (198,120 sq mi), Thailand is the [50th-largest country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area) by total area.[^cite_note-CIA-2] Thailand comprises several distinct geographic regions, partly corresponding to the provincial groups. The north of the country is the mountainous area of the [Thai highlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_highlands), with the highest point being [Doi Inthanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_Inthanon) in the [Thanon Thong Chai Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanon_Thong_Chai_Range) at 2,565 metres (8,415 ft) above sea level. The northeast, [Isan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isan), consists of the [Khorat Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorat_Plateau), bordered to the east by the [Mekong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mekong) River. The centre of the country is dominated by the predominantly flat [Chao Phraya River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chao_Phraya_River) valley, which runs into the [Gulf of Thailand](198107.md). Southern Thailand consists of the narrow [Kra Isthmus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kra_Isthmus) that widens into the [Malay Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Peninsula).

The Chao Phraya and the Mekong River are the indispensable water courses of rural Thailand. Industrial-scale production of crops uses both rivers and their tributaries. The Gulf of Thailand covers 320,000 square kilometres (124,000 sq mi) and is fed by the Chao Phraya, [Mae Klong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mae_Klong_River), [Bang Pakong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bang_Pakong_River), and [Tapi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapi_River,_Thailand) Rivers. It contributes to the tourism sector owing to its clear shallow waters along the coasts in the southern region and the Kra Isthmus. The eastern shore has the kingdom's premier deepwater port in [Sattahip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sattahip_district) and its busiest commercial port, [Laem Chabang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laem_Chabang). [Phuket](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phuket_Province), [Krabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krabi_Province), [Ranong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ranong), [Phang Nga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phang_Nga) and [Trang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trang_Province), and their islands, all lie along the coasts of the [Andaman Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andaman_Sea) and host luxurious resorts.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148]

### Climate

Map of Thailand colour-coded to illustrate tropical savanna, monsoon, and rainforest climate regions.

Thailand map of Köppen climate classification

The climate is influenced by [monsoon](57630.md) winds that have a seasonal character (the southwest and northeast monsoon).[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 2 </sup> Most of the country is classified as [Köppen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification)'s [tropical savanna climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_savanna_climate).[^cite_note-150] The majority of the south, as well as the eastern tip, have a [tropical monsoon climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_monsoon_climate). Parts of the south also have a [tropical rainforest climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_rainforest_climate). A year in Thailand is divided into three seasons.[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 2 </sup> The first is the rainy or southwest monsoon season (mid–May to mid–October), which is caused by southwestern wind from the [Indian Ocean](14580.md).[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 2 </sup>

Rainfall is also contributed by [Intertropical Convergence Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intertropical_Convergence_Zone) and tropical [cyclones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyclones),[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 2 </sup> with August and September being the wettest period of the year.[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 2 </sup> The country receives a mean annual rainfall of 1,200 to 1,600 mm (47 to 63 in).[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 4 </sup> Winter or the northeast monsoon occurs from mid-October until mid-February.[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 2 </sup> Most of Thailand experiences dry weather with mild temperatures.[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 2, 4 </sup> Summer or the pre-monsoon season runs from mid-February until mid-May.[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 3 </sup>

Due to their inland position and latitude, the north, northeast, central and eastern parts of Thailand experience a long period of warm weather, where temperatures can reach up to 40 °C (104 °F) during March to May,[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 3 </sup> in contrast to close to or below 0 °C (32 °F) in some areas in winter.[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 3 </sup> Southern Thailand is characterised by mild weather year-round with less diurnal and seasonal variations in temperatures due to maritime influences.[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 3 </sup> It receives abundant rainfall, particularly during October to November.[^cite_note-Climate_2015-149]<sup>: 2 </sup> Thailand is among the world's ten countries that are most exposed to [climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Thailand). In particular, it is highly vulnerable to [rising sea levels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level_rise) and extreme weather events.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152]

### Biodiversity and conservation

An Asian elephant standing among dense, green rainforest foliage.

The population of [Asian elephants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_elephants) in Thailand's wild has dropped to an estimated 2,000–3,000.

[^cite_note-Elephants-153]

[National parks in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_parks_of_Thailand) are defined as *an area that contains natural resources of ecological importance or unique beauty, or flora and fauna of special importance*. [Protected areas include](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_protected_areas_of_Thailand) 156 national parks, 58 wildlife sanctuaries, 67 non-hunting areas, and [120 forest parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_forest_parks_of_Thailand). They cover almost 31 per cent of the country.[^cite_note-PRD-154] The parks are administered by the [National Parks, Wildlife and Plant Conservation Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_National_Parks,_Wildlife_and_Plant_Conservation) of the [Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Natural_Resources_and_Environment_\(Thailand\)). The [Khao Kheow Open Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khao_Kheow_Open_Zoo) in [Chonburi](47777263.md) is the second largest zoo in Asia.[^cite_note-cnbctv1-155] The largest national park is [Kaeng Krachan National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaeng_Krachan_National_Park).

Reflecting on various [environmental issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Thailand), Thailand has a mediocre but improving performance in the global [Environmental Performance Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_Performance_Index), with an overall ranking of 91 out of 180 countries in 2016. The environmental areas where Thailand performs worst (i.e., highest-ranking) are [air quality](10934212.md) (167), environmental effects of the agricultural industry (106), and the [climate and energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_and_energy) sector (93), the latter mainly because of a high [CO<sub>2</sub> emission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CO2_emission) per kWh produced. Thailand performs best (i.e., lowest-ranking) in [water resource management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_resource_management) (66), with some major improvements expected for the future, and [sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitation) (68).[^cite_note-156][^cite_note-157] The country had a 2019 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index) mean score of 6.00/10, ranking it 88th globally out of 172 countries.[^cite_note-158]

The population of elephants, the [national symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_animals), has fallen from 100,000 in 1850 to an estimated 2,000.[^cite_note-Elephants-153] Poachers have long hunted elephants for [ivory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivory) and hides, and now increasingly for [meat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elephant_meat).[^cite_note-159] Young elephants are often captured for use in tourist attractions or as work animals, where there have been claims of mistreatment.[^cite_note-160] In 1989, the government banned the use of elephants for [logging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logging), leading many elephant owners to move their domesticated animals to the tourism industry.[^cite_note-161]

Poaching of [protected species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_species_native_to_Thailand) remains a major problem. [Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tigers), [leopards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopards), and other large cats are hunted for their pelts. Many are farmed or hunted for their meat, which supposedly has medicinal properties. Although such trade is illegal, the well-known Bangkok market [Chatuchak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatuchak_Weekend_Market) is still known for the sale of endangered species.[^cite_note-162] The practice of keeping wild animals as pets affects species such as [Asiatic black bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiatic_black_bear), [Malayan sun bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayan_sun_bear), [white-handed lar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lar_gibbon), [pileated gibbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pileated_gibbon), and [binturong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binturong).[^cite_note-163]

## Politics and government

Main articles: [Politics of Thailand](30132.md) and [Government of Thailand](19068660.md)

Further information: [Constitutions of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutions_of_Thailand) and [Elections in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Thailand)

Portrait of King Vajiralongkorn in official uniform

[Vajiralongkorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajiralongkorn)\
[King of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Thailand)\
since 13 October 2016

Portrait of Prime Minister Anutin Charnvirakul

[Anutin Charnvirakul](60867789.md)\
[Prime Minister of Thailand](273644.md)\
since 7 September 2025

Prior to 1932, Thai kings were feudal or [absolute monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy). During the Sukhothai Kingdom, the king was seen as a *dharmaraja*, or a 'king who rules in accordance with [dharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma)'. The system of government was a network of tributaries ruled by local lords. Modern absolute monarchy and statehood were established by King Chulalongkorn when he transformed the decentralised protectorate system into a unitary state. On 24 June 1932, the [Khana Ratsadon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khana_Ratsadon) (People's Party) carried out a bloodless revolution that marked the beginning of a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy).

Thailand has had 20 constitutions and charters since 1932, including the current 2017 constitution. All constitutions state that politics are conducted within the framework of a constitutional monarchy, but the *de facto* form of government has ranged from military dictatorship to electoral democracy.[^cite_note-multiple-164][^cite_note-165] Thailand's current form of government is a hybrid of democracy and dictatorship; many terms are used to describe it.[^cite_note-170] Thailand has had the fourth-most [coups](25914154.md) in the world.[^cite_note-171] "Uniformed or ex-military men have led Thailand for 55 of the 83 years" between 1932 and 2009.[^cite_note-172] Most recently, a [military junta](216815.md) calling itself the [National Council for Peace and Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_for_Peace_and_Order) ruled the country between 2014 and 2019.

Panoramic view of the exterior of Sappaya-Sapasathan, the current Parliament House of Thailand.

[Sappaya-Sapasathan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sappaya-Sapasathan) is the current Parliament House of Thailand

Government is [separated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_of_powers) into three branches:

- **The legislative branch:** The [National Assembly](14846736.md) is composed of the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Thailand), an indirectly elected 200-member [upper house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_house), and the [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Representatives_of_Thailand), a directly elected 500-member [lower house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_house). Its most recent election was [the 2026 general election](74706351.md). A coalition led by the [Bhumjaithai Party](20737916.md) currently holds the majority. The [2024 Thai Senate election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Thai_Senate_election) was the first senate election held under the current constitution through a process criticised as "the most complicated election in the world".

  [^cite_note-173]

  &#x20;The Senate is allegedly dominated by Bhumjaithai Party-affiliated senators.

  [^cite_note-174]

- **The executive branch:** Consists of the [Prime Minister of Thailand](273644.md), who serves as the [head of government](37570.md), and a cabinet of up to 35 members. The prime minister is elected by the House of Representatives. The current constitution mandates that the prime minister must be considered from candidates nominated by political parties before the election. The current prime minister is [Anutin Charnvirakul](60867789.md), a member of the Bhumjaithai Party.

- **The judiciary:** Is nominally independent of the executive and legislative branches, although judicial rulings are often suspected of being based on political considerations rather than on existing law.

  [^cite_note-175]

Military and bureaucratic aristocrats fully controlled political parties between 1946 and the 1980s.[^cite_note-176]<sup>: 16 </sup> Most parties in Thailand are short-lived.[^cite_note-Croissant_2010-177]<sup>: 246 </sup> Between 1992 and 2006, Thailand had a [two-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two-party_system).[^cite_note-Croissant_2010-177]<sup>: 245 </sup> Later constitutions created a multi-party system in which a single party cannot easily gain a majority in the House.

A [hereditary monarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hereditary_monarchy) serves as [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state). The current King of Thailand is [Vajiralongkorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajiralongkorn) (Rama X), who has reigned since October 2016. The powers of the king are limited by the constitution, and he is primarily a symbolic figurehead. However, the monarch still occasionally intervenes in politics, as all constitutions pave the way for customary royal rulings. Some academics outside Thailand, including [Duncan McCargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duncan_McCargo) and Federico Ferrara, note the extraconstitutional role of the monarch through a "[network monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_monarchy)" behind the political scenes.[^cite_note-178]

Chart displaying the chronological timeline of Thailand's Prime Ministers.

Timeline of Prime Ministers

The monarchy is protected by a severe [*lèse majesté* law](35397243.md), even though the public's attitude towards the institution varies from one reign to another.[^cite_note-179][^cite_note-180] *Lèse-majesté* laws allow critics to be jailed for three to fifteen years.[^cite_note-181] After the [coup d'état in 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Thai_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), Thailand had the highest [number of *lèse-majesté* prisoners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:People_accused_of_l%C3%A8se_majest%C3%A9_in_Thailand) in the nation's history.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] [Human rights in Thailand](4317552.md) have been rated *[not free](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_in_the_World_\(report\))* on the [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) Index since 2014.[^cite_note-184]

In 2024, the [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Thailand) banned the victors of the [2023 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Thai_general_election), the [Move Forward Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Move_Forward_Party), and all of its leaders from politics over their proposal to reform the *lèse-majesté* law, arguing it posed a threat to the constitutional order.[^cite_note-Economist1-185] *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* criticised the move as an example of "[lawfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawfare)" and pointed to the dissolution of its predecessor party, [Future Forward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_Forward_Party), in 2020 as the latest example of how an "alliance of conservative forces in Thailand—including monarchists, the army, and a handful of business tycoons—has sought to suppress opposition".[^cite_note-Economist1-185][^cite_note-186]

In the [Freedom in the World 2024 Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House), the country's status improved from *not free* to *[partly free](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_in_the_World)* due to competitive parliamentary elections and the formation of a new governing coalition by what had been a major opposition party, though unelected senators ensured that the party with the most votes was excluded.[^cite_note-187]

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [Organization of the government of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_the_government_of_Thailand), [Provinces of Thailand](204637.md), and [Administrative Divisions of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_Divisions_of_Thailand)

See also: [Regions of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Thailand)

Thailand is a [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state); the administrative services of the executive branch are divided into three levels by the *National Government Organisation Act, BE 2534* (1991): central, provincial, and local. Thailand is composed of 76 [provinces](204637.md) (จังหวัด, *changwat*),[^cite_note-188] which are first-level [administrative divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_division). There are also two specially governed districts: the capital Bangkok and [Pattaya](712796.md). Bangkok is at the provincial level and is thus often counted as a province.[^cite_note-CapitalCity-15] Each province is divided into [districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Districts_of_Thailand) (อำเภอ, *amphoe*) and these districts are further divided into sub-districts (ตำบล, *tambons*). The name of each province's capital city (เมือง, mueang) is the same as that of the province. For example, the capital of [Chiang Mai Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Mai_Province) (*Changwat Chiang Mai*) is *Mueang Chiang Mai* or *Chiang Mai*. All provincial governors and district chiefs, who are the administrators of provinces and districts respectively, are appointed by the central government.[^cite_note-189] The provinces are sometimes grouped into four to six regions, depending on the source.

A clickable map of Thailand exhibiting its provinces

A clickable map of Thailand exhibiting its provinces

A clickable map of Thailand exhibiting its provinces

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Thailand)

King Bhumibol Adulyadej and US President Barack Obama sitting together during a meeting.

King [Bhumibol Adulyadej](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhumibol_Adulyadej) in a meeting with US President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama), 18 November 2012

Siam and Thailand's approach to conducting foreign relations has long been described as "bamboo bending with the wind"—a policy that is "always solidly rooted, but flexible enough to bend whichever way the wind blows in order to survive"[^cite_note-190]—or simply as adaptable and pragmatic. To secure its independence, the nation historically sought to balance great powers against one another so that it would be dominated by none.[^cite_note-191]

During the [Cold War](325329.md), Thailand sought to prevent the spread of communism by aligning with the United States. This included participating in the [SEATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEATO) alliance, sending expeditionary forces to Korea and Vietnam, and allowing the US to use its military bases. Thailand is one of the five founding members of the [Association of Southeast Asian Nations](106539.md) (ASEAN), which was initially formed to safeguard against communism. The end of the [Vietnam War](32611.md) marked a turning point in Thai foreign policy, after which the country sought to improve relations with communist China and its regional neighbours. Thailand remains an active member of ASEAN and seeks to project its influence within the bloc. It has developed increasingly close ties with other members, progressing regional cooperation in economic, trade, banking, political, and cultural matters.[^cite_note-192]

In the 2000s, Thailand took an active role on the international stage, participating fully in international and regional organisations. It is designated as a [major non-NATO ally](3812445.md) and is on the Priority Watch List of the United States' [Special 301 Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_301_Report). When [East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Timor) gained independence from Indonesia, Thailand contributed troops to the international peacekeeping effort.[^cite_note-193] As part of its effort to expand international ties, Thailand has also reached out to regional organisations such as the [Organization of American States](22788.md) (OAS)[^cite_note-194] and the [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](22796.md) (OSCE).[^cite_note-195]

During [Thaksin Shinawatra](360815.md)'s premiership, negotiations for several [free trade agreements](2533562.md) with China, Australia, Bahrain, India, and the US were initiated. Thaksin sought to position Thailand as a regional leader by spearheading development projects in poorer neighbouring countries. More controversially, he established close, friendly ties with the Burmese dictatorship.[^cite_note-196] Thailand joined the US-led [invasion of Iraq](201936.md), contributing a [humanitarian contingent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_Humanitarian_Assistance_Task_Force_976_Thai-Iraq) until September 2004.[^cite_note-197] Thailand also contributed troops to reconstruction efforts in [Afghanistan](737.md).[^cite_note-198]

In April 2009, the [2008–2013 Cambodian–Thai border crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008%E2%80%932013_Cambodian%E2%80%93Thai_border_crisis) led to troop deployments on territory immediately adjacent to the 900-year-old ruins of Cambodia's [Preah Vihear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preah_Vihear_Temple) Hindu temple near the border.[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200]

Following the 2014 coup, Thailand began leaning more heavily towards China.[^cite_note-201] Growing Chinese influence and capital inflows have caused some members of parliament to raise concerns about Thailand becoming an "economic colony" as a result of numerous concessions.[^cite_note-202]

During the 2023 [Israel–Gaza war](74998519.md), Thailand's prime minister initially stated that his government strongly condemned the attacks against Israel, extending his deepest condolences to the Israeli government and people.[^cite_note-203] However, the Thai government later adjusted its position, announcing a neutral stance in the conflict.[^cite_note-204] Twenty-eight Thai nationals were killed in the conflict.[^cite_note-205]

### Armed forces

Aerial view of the HTMS Chakri Naruebet aircraft carrier at sea.

The [HTMS *Chakri Naruebet*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HTMS_Chakri_Naruebet), an [aircraft carrier](2219.md) of the [Royal Thai Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_Navy)

The [Royal Thai Armed Forces](30136.md) (กองทัพไทย; [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription): *Kong Thap Thai*) consist of the [Royal Thai Army](1230371.md) (กองทัพบกไทย), the [Royal Thai Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_Navy) (กองทัพเรือไทย), and the [Royal Thai Air Force](1778758.md) (กองทัพอากาศไทย). They also incorporate various [paramilitary](146730.md) forces.[^cite_note-CIA-2]

The [head of the Thai Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Thailand) (จอมทัพไทย, *Chom Thap Thai*) is the king,[^cite_note-206] although this position is only nominal. The armed forces are managed by the [Ministry of Defence of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_\(Thailand\)), which is headed by the Minister of Defence (a member of the [cabinet of Thailand](7366930.md)) and commanded by the [Royal Thai Armed Forces Headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_Armed_Forces_Headquarters), which in turn is headed by the [Chief of Defence Forces of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Commanders_of_the_Royal_Thai_Armed_Forces_Headquarters).[^cite_note-207] The annual defence budget almost tripled from US\$1.98 billion in 2005 to US\$5.88 billion in 2016, accounting for approximately 1.4% of GDP.[^cite_note-World_Bank_Open_Data-208] Thailand ranked 16th worldwide in the Military Strength Index based on the [Credit Suisse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_Suisse) report in 2015.[^cite_note-CreditSuisse2015-209]

A JAS 39 Gripen military aircraft flying in the sky.

A Royal Thai Air Force [JAS 39 Gripen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JAS_39_Gripen)

The military is also tasked with humanitarian missions, such as escorting [Rohingya](1918632.md) to Malaysia or Indonesia, and ensuring security and welfare for refugees during the [Indochina refugee crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indochina_refugee_crisis).[^cite_note-210]

According to the constitution, serving in the armed forces is a duty of all citizens,[^cite_note-211] with an active draft system for males over age 21. They are subjected to varying lengths of active service depending on the duration of their reserve training as [Territorial Defence Students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_Defence_Student) and their level of education. Those who have completed three years or more of reserve training are exempted entirely. The practice has long been criticised, as some media outlets question its efficacy and value.[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213] It is alleged that conscripts often end up as servants to senior officers[^cite_note-214] or clerks in military cooperative shops.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216] In a report issued in March 2020, [Amnesty International](18947898.md) charged that military conscripts face institutionalised abuse that is systematically hushed up by military authorities.[^cite_note-217]

Critics have observed that the military's main objective is to deal with internal rather than external threats.[^cite_note-Asia_Sentinel_2017-218] The [Internal Security Operations Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Security_Operations_Command) is often called the political arm of the military, having overlapping social and political functions with the civilian bureaucracy. It also has an anti-democracy mission.[^cite_note-Asia_Sentinel_2017-218] The military is also notorious for numerous corruption incidents, such as accusations of [human trafficking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_trafficking_in_Thailand),[^cite_note-219] and [nepotism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepotism) in the promotion of high-ranking officers.[^cite_note-220] The military is deeply entrenched in politics. Most recently, the appointed senators included more than 100 active and retired military personnel.[^cite_note-221]

Thailand is the 75th most peaceful country in the world, according to the 2024 [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index).[^cite_note-222]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Thailand)

| Nominal [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP) | ฿14.53 trillion  (2016)[^cite_note-223] |
| --- | --- |
| GDP growth | 3.9%  (2017)[^cite_note-NESDB_2561-224] |
| Headline inflation | 0.7%  (2017)[^cite_note-NESDB_2561-224] |
| Core inflation | 0.6%  (2017)[^cite_note-NESDB_2561-224] |
| [Employment-to-population ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment-to-population_ratio) | 68.0%  (2017)[^cite_note-NESDB_2560-225]<sup>: 29 </sup> |
| Unemployment | 1.2%  (2017)[^cite_note-NESDB_2561-224] |
| Total public debt | ฿6.37 trillion  (Dec. 2017)[^cite_note-226] |
| Poverty | 8.61%  (2016)[^cite_note-NESDB_2560-225]<sup>: 36 </sup> |
| Net household worth | ฿20.34 trillion  (2010)[^cite_note-227]<sup>: 2 </sup> |

A dense cityscape featuring numerous high-rise skyscrapers in the Sathorn business district of Bangkok, seen during daytime.

[Sathorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sathorn) in Bangkok is a skyscraper-studded business district that is home to major hotels and embassies.

The economy of Thailand is heavily export-dependent, with exports accounting for more than two-thirds of [gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP). Thailand exports over US\$105 billion worth of goods and services annually.[^cite_note-CIA-2] Major exports include cars, computers, electrical appliances, [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_production_in_Thailand), textiles and footwear, fishery products, rubber, and jewellery.[^cite_note-CIA-2]

Thailand is an [emerging economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_markets) and is considered a [newly industrialised country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newly_industrialised_country). Thailand had a 2026 GDP of US\$1.964 trillion (on a [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity) basis).[^cite_note-IMFWEO.TH-6] It is the second-largest economy in Southeast Asia after Indonesia, and ranks midway in the region's wealth spread as the fourth-richest nation by GDP per capita.

Thailand functions as an [anchor economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anchor_economy) for the neighbouring developing economies of Laos, Myanmar, and Cambodia. In the third quarter of 2014, the unemployment rate stood at 0.84% according to Thailand's National Economic and Social Development Board.[^cite_note-228] In 2017, the economy grew by an inflation-adjusted 3.9%, up from 3.3% in 2016, marking its fastest expansion since 2012.[^cite_note-229] High public spending, especially during the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Thailand), prompted the authorities to raise the public debt ceiling from 60% to 70% of GDP.[^cite_note-230]

As of 2024, Thailand struggles with low productivity, poor education, high household debt, low private investment, and slow economic growth,[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232] with one economic research group forecasting an annual GDP growth of below 2% in the coming decades without structural reforms.[^cite_note-233]

In 2025, the [Bank of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Thailand)'s economic outlook page projected GDP growth of around 2.2% in 2025 and 1.6% in 2026. The bank noted that growth in the first half of 2025 was supported by front‑loaded exports to the U.S. ahead of tariff hikes, while the second half was expected to slow as higher U.S. tariffs took effect.[^cite_note-234]

### Income and wealth disparities

A line graph showing the historical development and steady growth of Thailand's real GDP per capita from 1890 to 2022.

Development of real GDP per capita, 1890 to 2022

The median wealth per Thai adult was \$1,469 in 2016,[^cite_note-Wealth_2016-235]<sup>: 98 </sup> increasing from \$605 in 2010.[^cite_note-Wealth_2016-235]<sup>: 34 </sup> In 2016, Thailand was [ranked 87th in the Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index) and [70th in the inequality-adjusted HDI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_inequality-adjusted_HDI).[^cite_note-236]
