In 2017, the median household income was ฿26,946 per month.[^cite_note-NSO_2560-237]<sup>: 1 </sup> The top quintile of households had a 45.0% share of all income, while the bottom quintile had 7.1%.[^cite_note-NSO_2560-237]<sup>: 4 </sup> An estimated 26.9 million people in the bottom 40% of the income bracket earned less than ฿5,344 per person per month.[^cite_note-NESDB_2559-238]<sup>: 5 </sup> During the [2013–2014 Thai political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013%E2%80%932014_Thai_political_crisis), a survey found that the largest portion of anti-government [PDRC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Democratic_Reform_Committee) supporters (32%) had a monthly income of more than ฿50,000, while the largest portion of pro-government [UDD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Front_for_Democracy_Against_Dictatorship) supporters (27%) earned between ฿10,000 and ฿20,000.[^cite_note-239]<sup>: 7 </sup>

In 2014, [Credit Suisse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_Suisse) reported that Thailand was the world's third-most unequal country, behind Russia and India.[^cite_note-BBC_2017-240] The wealthiest 10% held 79% of the country's assets.[^cite_note-BBC_2017-240] The top 1% held 58% of the assets.[^cite_note-BBC_2017-240] The 50 richest Thai families had a total net worth amounting to 30% of GDP.[^cite_note-BBC_2017-240] The [Bank of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_Thailand) reported that between 2006 and 2016, Thailand's largest 5% of companies accounted for 85% of all corporate revenue in the nation, and only 6% of the country's companies were in export industries, which made up 60% of the country's GDP.[^cite_note-241]

In 2016, 5.81 million people lived in poverty, or 11.6 million people (17.2% of the population) if the "near poor" are included.[^cite_note-NESDB_2559-238]<sup>: 1 </sup> The proportion of the poor relative to the total population in each region was 12.96% in the Northeast, 12.35% in the South, and 9.83% in the North.[^cite_note-NESDB_2559-238]<sup>: 2 </sup> In 2017, 14 million people applied for social welfare (which required a yearly income of less than ฿100,000).[^cite_note-BBC_2017-240] In the first quarter of 2023, Thai household debt totalled 14.6 trillion baht or 89.2% of GDP; the average debt per household was approximately 500,000 baht.[^cite_note-242] In 2016, there were an estimated 30,000 homeless people in the country.[^cite_note-243]

### Exports and manufacturing

In 2022, Thailand's exports of goods were worth roughly US\$290 billion while its imports were worth roughly US\$305 billion.[^cite_note-244] Recovery from the 1997–1998 Asian financial crisis relied heavily on exports. As of 2025, the [Thai automotive industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automotive_industry_in_Thailand) was the largest in Southeast Asia and the [12th-largest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_motor_vehicle_production).[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246][^cite_note-OICA_2013-247] The Thai industry has an annual output of nearly 1.5 million vehicles, mostly commercial vehicles.[^cite_note-OICA_2013-247]

Most of the vehicles built in Thailand are developed and licensed by foreign producers, mainly Japanese and American. The car industry takes advantage of the [ASEAN Free Trade Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASEAN_Free_Trade_Area) (AFTA) to find a market for many of its products. Eight manufacturers—five Japanese, two American, and India's [Tata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tata_Group)—produce pick-up trucks in Thailand.[^cite_note-Takahashi_2014-248] As of 2012, due to its favourable taxation for 2-door pick-ups at only 3–12% against 17–50% for passenger cars, Thailand was the second-largest consumer of pick-up trucks in the world, after the US.[^cite_note-249] In 2014, pick-ups accounted for 42% of all new vehicle sales in Thailand.[^cite_note-Takahashi_2014-248]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Thailand](6188546.md)

The towering, ornately decorated spires of Wat Arun, a famous Buddhist temple complex on the riverside in Bangkok.

[Wat Arun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat_Arun), [Bangkok](56615.md), is among the best-known Thailand's landmarks.

Tourism makes up about 6% of the economy. Prior to the pandemic, Thailand was the world's eighth-most visited country according to the [World Tourism rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Tourism_rankings) compiled by the [United Nations World Tourism Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_World_Tourism_Organization). In 2019, Thailand received 39.8 million international tourists, ahead of the [United Kingdom](31717.md) and [Germany](11867.md)[^cite_note-250] and ranked fourth globally in international tourism receipts, earning 60.5 billion [US dollars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_dollars).

Thailand was the most visited country in Southeast Asia in 2013, according to the World Tourism Organisation. Estimates of direct tourism receipts contributing to the Thai GDP of 12 trillion baht range from 9 per cent (1 trillion baht in 2013) to 16 per cent.[^cite_note-251] When factoring in the indirect effects of tourism, the sector is estimated to account for 20.2 per cent (2.4 trillion baht) of Thailand's GDP.[^cite_note-252]<sup>: 1 </sup>

Asian tourists primarily visit Thailand for Bangkok and the historical, natural, and cultural sights in its vicinity. Western tourists visit Bangkok and surrounding areas, as well as the southern beaches and islands. The north is the chief destination for trekking and [adventure travel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adventure_travel), offering diverse ethnic minority groups and forested mountains. The region hosting the fewest tourists is Isan.[^cite_note-253]

Thailand ranks as the world's fifth-largest [medical tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_tourism) destination in spending, according to the [World Travel and Tourism Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Travel_and_Tourism_Council), attracting over 2.5 million visitors in 2018,[^cite_note-254] and is number one in Asia.[^cite_note-255] The country is popular for the growing practice of [sex reassignment surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_reassignment_surgery) and cosmetic surgery.[^cite_note-256] [Prostitution in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prostitution_in_Thailand) and sex tourism also form a *de facto* part of the economy. Campaigns promote Thailand as exotic to attract tourists.[^cite_note-257] One estimate published in 2003 placed the trade at US\$4.3 billion per year, or about 3% of the Thai economy.[^cite_note-258] It is believed that at least 10% of tourist dollars are spent on the sex trade.[^cite_note-259]

### Agriculture and natural resources

Further information: [Agriculture in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Thailand)

Farmers wearing traditional conical hats working in a lush green rice paddy in Mae Wang, Chiang Mai.

Thailand has long been [one of the largest rice exporters in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice_production_in_Thailand). Forty-nine per cent of Thailand's labour force is employed in agriculture, down from 70% in 1980.

[^cite_note-Leturque_2010-260]

Forty-nine per cent of the labour force is employed in agriculture, down from 70% in 1980.[^cite_note-Leturque_2010-260] Rice is the most important crop, and Thailand had long been the world's leading exporter of rice, although it has recently fallen behind India and Vietnam.[^cite_note-261] Thailand has the highest percentage of arable land, 27.25%, of any state in the [Greater Mekong Subregion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Mekong_Subregion).[^cite_note-262] About 55% of the arable land area is used for rice production.[^cite_note-263]

Agriculture has been experiencing a transition from labour-intensive and traditional methods to a more industrialised and competitive sector.[^cite_note-Leturque_2010-260] Between 1962 and 1983, the agricultural sector grew by 4.1% per year on average, and continued to grow at 2.2% between 1983 and 2007.[^cite_note-Leturque_2010-260] The relative contribution of agriculture to GDP has declined, while exports of goods and services have increased.

Access to [biocapacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biocapacity) is lower than the world average, with 1.2 global hectares[^cite_note-Global_Footprint_Network-264] of biocapacity per person, compared to the world average of 1.6.[^cite_note-265] In 2016, Thailand used 2.5 global hectares—the [ecological footprint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_footprint) of consumption. This means the country uses about twice as much biocapacity as it contains, resulting in a deficit.[^cite_note-Global_Footprint_Network-264]

### Informal economy

An aerial nighttime view of a bustling market with hundreds of brightly colored, illuminated vendor tents clustered closely together.

[The Train Night Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talat_Rotfai) in Bangkok

In 2012, it was estimated that informal workers comprised 62.6% of the workforce. The [Ministry of Labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Labour_\(Thailand\)) defines informal workers as individuals who work in the informal economy and lack employee status under the country's Labour Protection Act. The informal sector has grown significantly over the past 60 years during Thailand's gradual transition from an agricultural economy to a more industrialised and service-oriented one.[^cite_note-Kongtip-2015-266] Between 1993 and 1995, 10 per cent of the labour force moved from the agricultural sector to urban and industrial jobs, especially in the manufacturing sector. It is estimated that between 1988 and 1995, the number of factory workers doubled from two to four million, as the GDP tripled.[^cite_note-267]

&#x20;While the [Asian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Asian_financial_crisis) that followed in 1997 hit the economy hard, the industrial sector continued to expand under widespread deregulation, as Thailand was mandated to adopt a range of structural adjustment reforms upon receiving funding from the IMF and World Bank. These reforms implemented an agenda of increased privatisation and trade liberalisation, alongside decreased state subsidisation of public goods and utilities, agricultural price supports, and regulations on fair wages and labour conditions.[^cite_note-Guille_2014-268] Many migrant farmers took jobs in [sweatshops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweatshop) and factories with few labour regulations and often exploitative conditions.[^cite_note-269] Those who could not find formal factory work, including undocumented migrants and the families of rural Thai migrants, entered the informal sector shaped by these structural adjustment programmes. Scholars argue that the economic consequences and social costs of Thailand's labour reforms in the wake of the 1997 Asian financial crisis fell on individuals and families rather than the state.[^cite_note-Guille_2014-268]&#x20;

People gathered outdoors near an ancient brick city wall in Chiang Mai, participating in water fights during the Songkran festival.

[Songkran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songkran_\(Thailand\)) Festival, [Chiang Mai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Mai)'s ancient city wall

Informal workers in entertainment, nightlife, and the sex industry face additional vulnerabilities, including recruitment into circles of sexual exploitation and human trafficking.[^cite_note-Kongtip-2015-266] A 2012 study found that 64% of informal workers had not completed education beyond primary school. Many informal workers are also migrants, only some of whom have legal status in the country. The informal labour sector is also not recognised under the Labour Protection Act. Social security policies fail to provide protection against workplace accidents, or offer unemployment and retirement insurance. Many informal workers are not legally contracted for their employment, and many do not make a living wage.[^cite_note-Kongtip-2015-266] Tens of thousands of migrants from neighbouring countries face exploitation in a few industries,[^cite_note-270] especially in fishing, where slave-like conditions have been reported.[^cite_note-271]

### Science and technology

Thailand ranked 45th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273] The [Ministry of Higher Education, Science, Research and Innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Higher_Education,_Science,_Research_and_Innovation) and [its agencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Higher_Education,_Science,_Research_and_Innovation#Organization) oversee the development of science, technology, and research. According to the National Research Council of Thailand, the country devoted 1.1% of its GDP to the research and development of science in 2019, with over 166,788 full-time equivalent research and development personnel that year.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275]

## Infrastructure

### Transportation

Main article: [Transport in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Thailand)

The elevated trains travelling along a concrete track flanked by modern high-rise city buildings.

The [BTS Skytrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BTS_Skytrain) is an elevated rapid transit system in Bangkok.

The [State Railway of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Railway_of_Thailand) (SRT) operates all national rail lines. [Krung Thep Aphiwat Central Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krung_Thep_Aphiwat_Central_Terminal) and [Bangkok (Hua Lamphong)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangkok_\(Hua_Lamphong\)_railway_station) are the main termini for intercity routes, while Phahonyothin and the [Lat Krabang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lat_Krabang) Inland Container Depot (ICD) are the primary freight terminals. As of 2025, the SRT had 4,507 km (2,801 mi) of track, all of it [metre gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meter_gauge). Nearly all of it is single-track (2,702.1 km (1,679.0 mi)), although key sections around Bangkok are double-tracked (1,234.9 km (767.3 mi)) or triple-tracked (107 km (66 mi)), with expansion plans underway.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277] [Rail transport in Bangkok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Bangkok) includes long-distance services as well as four rapid transit systems: the [BTS Skytrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BTS_Skytrain), [MRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MRT_\(Bangkok\)), [SRT Red Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SRT_Red_Lines), and [Airport Rail Link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airport_Rail_Link_\(Bangkok\)).[^cite_note-278]

Thailand has 390,000 kilometres (240,000 miles) of highways.[^cite_note-279] As of 2017, there were over 462,133 km of roads and 37 million registered vehicles, 20 million of which were motorcycles.[^cite_note-280] Many undivided two-lane highways have been converted into divided four-lane highways. Within the Bangkok Metropolitan Region, there are numerous [controlled-access highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled-access_highways_in_Thailand). In Bangkok alone, 4,125 public vans operate on 114 routes.[^cite_note-281] Other forms of road transport include [tuk-tuks](593765.md), taxis (with over 80,647 registered nationwide as of 2018),[^cite_note-282] passenger vans, motorcycle taxis, and [songthaews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songthaew).

As of 2012, Thailand had 103 airports with 63 paved runways, in addition to six heliports. The busiest airport is Bangkok's [Suvarnabhumi Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suvarnabhumi_Airport).[^cite_note-283]

### Energy

Main article: [Energy in Thailand](5515384.md)

In 2014, natural gas generated 75% of Thailand's electricity.[^cite_note-Energy_2013-284] Coal-fired power plants produced an additional 20%, with the remainder coming from biomass, hydro, and biogas.[^cite_note-Energy_2013-284] Compared to other ASEAN countries, Thailand is the largest importer of gas by weight.[^cite_note-ASEAN_Centre_for_Energy-2023-285]<sup>: 17 </sup> In 2022, oil and gas production dropped by 19% and 17%, respectively.[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-ASEAN_Centre_for_Energy-2023-285]<sup>: 8 </sup> In 2018, the government introduced the Alternative Energy Development Plan 2018–2037 (AEDP 2018), which aims to increase renewable energy capacity to nearly 30,000 MW by 2037.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288]

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Thailand)

Ethnic groups of Thailand\
(2015 estimate by [The World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Factbook))

[^cite_note-CIA-2]

1. [Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_people) (97.5%)

2. [Burmese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burmese_in_Thailand) (1.30%)

3. other (1.10%)

4. unspecified (0.10%)

Thailand had a population of 65.95 million in 2025.[^cite_note-Khaosod_English-2025-289] There were 64,953,661 Thai nationals and 997,549 non-Thai residents.[^cite_note-Khaosod_English-2025-289] Thailand's first census in 1909 found the population to be 8.2 million.[^cite_note-290] The population is largely rural, concentrated in the rice-growing areas of the central, northeastern, and northern regions. About 44.2% of the population lived in urban areas as of 2010, a gradual increase from 29.4% in the 1990 census and 31.1% in the 2000 census.[^cite_note-291]

The government-sponsored family planning programme resulted in a dramatic decline in population growth, from 3.1% in 1960 to around 0.4% today. In 1970, an average of 5.7 people lived in a Thai household; in 2022, the average household size was three people.[^cite_note-292] Currently, more than 20% of the population is over 60. This aging demographic, combined with a low birth rate, poses economic challenges.[^cite_note-293] The [sex ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_ratio) of males to females is 1.05.[^cite_note-294] The [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) declined to 1.0 in 2024,[^cite_note-chairith-295][^cite_note-Khaosod_English-2025-289][^cite_note-\:1-296] placing Thailand among countries with [ultra-low fertility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sub-replacement_fertility) rates.[^cite_note-chairith-295][^cite_note-Khaosod_English-2025-289][^cite_note-\:1-296]

### Ethnic groups

Main article: [Ethnic groups in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Thailand)

Two young girls wearing vibrant, traditional embroidered hill tribe clothing.

Hill tribes girls in the Northeast of Thailand

As of 2010, Thai nationals make up the majority of the population (95.9%). The remaining 4.1% of the population are Burmese (2.0%), others (1.3%), and unspecified (0.9%).[^cite_note-CIA-2] According to genetic research, present-day Thai people are divided into three groups: the northern group (Khon Mueang) are closely related to the Tai ethnic groups in southern China, the northeastern group ([Isan people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isan_people)) are mixed Tai and several [Austroasiatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austroasiatic)-speaking ethnic groups, while the central and southern groups (formerly called Siamese) strongly share genetic profiles with the [Mon people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mon_people).[^cite_note-genetic-48]

According to the Royal Thai Government's 2011 Country Report to the UN Committee responsible for the [International Convention for the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Convention_on_the_Elimination_of_All_Forms_of_Racial_Discrimination), available from the Department of Rights and Liberties Promotion of the Thai Ministry of Justice, 62 ethnic communities are officially recognised in Thailand.[^cite_note-CERD/C/THA/1-3-297]<sup>: 3 </sup> Twenty million Central Thai (together with approximately 650,000 [Khorat Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorat_Thai)) made up approximately 20,650,000 (34.1 per cent) of the state's population of 60,544,937[^cite_note-298] at the time of completion of the Mahidol University *Ethnolinguistic Maps of Thailand* data (1997).[^cite_note-Ethnolinguistic_2004-299]

The 2011 Thailand Country Report provides population numbers for mountain peoples ('hill tribes') and ethnic communities in the Northeast and is explicit about its main reliance on the Mahidol University Ethnolinguistic Maps of Thailand data.[^cite_note-Ethnolinguistic_2004-299] Thus, though over 3.288 million people in the Northeast alone could not be categorised, the population and percentages of other ethnic communities c. 1997 are known for all of Thailand and constitute minimum populations. In descending order, the largest (equal to or greater than 400,000) are a) 15,080,000 Lao (24.9 per cent) consisting of the Thai Lao (14 million) and other smaller Lao groups, namely the Thai Loei (400–500,000), Lao Lom (350,000), Lao Wiang/Klang (200,000), Lao Khrang (90,000), Lao Ngaew (30,000), and Lao Ti (10,000); b) six million Khon Muang (9.9 per cent, also called Northern Thais); c) 4.5 million Pak Tai (7.5 per cent, also called Southern Thais); d) 1.4 million Khmer Leu (2.3 per cent, also called Northern Khmer); e) 900,000 Malay (1.5%); f) 500,000 Nyaw (0.8 per cent); g) 470,000 Phu Thai (0.8 per cent); h) 400,000 Kuy/Kuay (also known as Suay) (0.7 per cent), and i) 350,000 Karen (0.6 per cent).[^cite_note-CERD/C/THA/1-3-297]<sup>: 7–13 </sup> [Thai Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_Chinese), including those of significant Chinese origins, represent 14% of the population, while Thais with partial Chinese ancestry comprise up to 40% of the population.[^cite_note-300]

Increasing numbers of migrants from neighbouring Myanmar, Laos, and Cambodia, as well as from Nepal and India, have pushed the total number of non-national residents to around 3.5 million as of 2009, up from an estimated 2 million in 2008.[^cite_note-301] Some 41,000 [Britons](19097669.md) and 20,000 [Australians](11197621.md) live in Thailand.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303]

### Language

Main article: [Languages of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Thailand)

A map of Thailand color-coded by different ethnolinguistic groups

An ethnolinguistic map of Thailand

A tall, ancient stone pillar covered in engraved script standing in a museum

The Silajaruek of [Sukhothai Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukhothai_Kingdom) are hundreds of stone inscriptions that form a historical record of the period.

[Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language) is the official language. It is a [Kra–Dai language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kra%E2%80%93Dai_language) closely related to [Lao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lao_language), [Shan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shan_language) in Myanmar, and numerous smaller languages spoken in an arc from [Hainan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainan) and [Yunnan](166410.md) south to the Chinese border. It is the principal language of education and government and is spoken throughout the country.[^cite_note-Lewis-2009-304] The standard is based on the dialect of the central Thai people, and it is written in the [Thai alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_alphabet), an [abugida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abugida) script that evolved from the [Khmer alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_alphabet).[^cite_note-hartmann-305]

Sixty-two languages are recognised by the Royal Thai Government.[^cite_note-306] For the purposes of the national census, four dialects of Thai exist; these partly coincide with regional designations, such as [Southern Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Thai_language) and [Northern Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Thai_language).[^cite_note-Lewis-2009-304]

The largest of Thailand's minority languages is the [Lao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lao_language) dialect of [Isan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isan_language) spoken in the northeastern provinces. In the far south, [Kelantan-Pattani Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelantan-Pattani_Malay) is the primary language of Malay Muslims. Varieties of Chinese are also spoken by the large [Thai Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_Chinese) population, with the [Teochew dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_dialect) best-represented. Numerous tribal languages are also spoken, including many [Austroasiatic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austroasiatic_languages) such as [Mon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mon_language), [Khmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khmer_language), [Lawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawa_language) and [Mlabri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mlabri_language); [Austronesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages) such as [Cham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cham_language), [Moken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moken_language) and [Urak Lawoi'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urak_Lawoi'_language); [Sino-Tibetan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Tibetan_languages) like [Akha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akha_language) and [Karen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karen_languages); and other [Tai languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tai_languages) such as [Phu Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phu_Thai_language), and [Saek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saek_language). [Hmong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hmong_language) is a member of the [Hmong–Mien languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hmong%E2%80%93Mien_languages), which is now regarded as a language family of its own.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-Lewis-2009-304]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Thailand)

Religion in Thailand (2018)

[^cite_note-NSO-3]

1. [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Thailand) (93.5%)

2. [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Thailand) (5.37%)

3. [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Thailand) (1.13%)

4. [Other](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Thailand) (0.04%)

The country's most prevalent religion is [Theravada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada) Buddhism, which is an integral part of Thai identity and culture. Active participation in Buddhism is among the highest in the world. Thailand has the second-largest number of [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist) in the world after China.[^cite_note-308] According to the 2018 [National Statistical Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Statistical_Office_\(Thailand\)) data, 93.46% of the country's population self-identified as Buddhists.[^cite_note-NSO-3]

Buddhist monks in saffron robes standing before a large statue.

*[Samanera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samanera)* of [Theravada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada) Buddhism, the most practised religion in Thailand

[Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Thailand) constitute the second largest religious group in Thailand, comprising 5.37% of the population in 2018.[^cite_note-NSO-3] Islam is concentrated mostly in the country's southernmost provinces: [Pattani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pattani_Province), [Yala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yala_Province), [Satun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satun_Province), [Narathiwat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narathiwat_Province), and part of [Songkhla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songkhla_Province), which are predominantly [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malays_\(ethnic_group\)), most of whom are [Sunni Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Muslim). Christians represented 1.13% of the population in 2018, with the remaining population consisting of [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Thailand) and [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_Thailand), who live mostly in the country's cities. There is also a small [Jewish community in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_and_Judaism_in_Thailand) dating back to the 17th century.[^cite_note-309]

The constitution does not name an official state religion and provides for freedom of religion. There have been no widespread reports of societal abuses or discrimination based on religious belief or practice.[^cite_note-310] Thai law officially recognises five religious groups: Buddhists, Muslims, Brahmin-Hindus, Sikhs, and Christians.[^cite_note-311] However, some laws are inspired by Buddhist practices, such as banning alcohol sales on religious holidays.[^cite_note-312]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Thailand)

A traditional Thai-style building with an ornate multi-tiered roof reflecting in a pool of water.

[Chulalongkorn University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chulalongkorn_University), established in 1917, is the oldest university in Thailand.

In 1995, as minister of education, [Sukavich Rangsitpol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukavich_Rangsitpol) laid out his plans for educational reform in Thailand.[^cite_note-313] The reform was considered a landmark movement after nearly 100 years of education under the previous system.[^cite_note-314] Thailand's youth literacy rate was 98.1% in 2015.[^cite_note-315] Education is provided by a school system of kindergartens, primary, lower secondary and upper secondary schools, numerous vocational colleges, and universities. Education is compulsory up to and including age 14, while the government is mandated to provide free education through to age 17. Issues concerning university entrance have been in constant upheaval for a number of years. The country is also one of the few that mandates uniforms through the university level, a policy that remains a subject of ongoing debate.[^cite_note-316]

In 2013, the [Ministry of Information and Communication Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Information_and_Communication_Technology_\(Thailand\)) announced that 27,231 schools would receive classroom-level access to [high-speed internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet).[^cite_note-317] However, the country's educational infrastructure was still underprepared for online teaching, as smaller and more remote schools were particularly hindered by COVID-19 restrictions.[^cite_note-318]

The number of higher education institutions in Thailand has officially grown to 156 over the past decades. The two top-ranking universities in Thailand are [Chulalongkorn University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chulalongkorn_University) and [Mahidol University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahidol_University).[^cite_note-319] Thai universities' research output is still relatively low, even though the country's journal publications increased by 20% between 2011 and 2016.[^cite_note-320] Thailand has the second highest number of English-medium private international schools in [Southeast Asian Nations](106539.md).[^cite_note-WENR_2018-321] [Cram schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cram_school) are especially popular for university entrance exams.[^cite_note-322]

Students in ethnic minority areas score consistently lower in standardised national and international tests.[^cite_note-Draper_2012-323][^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325] This is likely due to an unequal allocation of educational resources, weak teacher training, poverty, and low skill in the Thai language, which is the language of the tests.[^cite_note-Draper_2012-323][^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327] As of 2020, Thailand was ranked 89th out of 100 countries globally for English proficiency.[^cite_note-328] Thailand is the third most popular study destination in ASEAN. The number of international degree students in Thailand increased by 9.7 times between 1999 and 2012, from 1,882 to 20,309 students. Most international students come from neighbouring countries[^cite_note-WENR_2018-321] like China, Myanmar, Cambodia and Vietnam.[^cite_note-329]

### Health

Main articles: [Health in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Thailand), [Healthcare in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Thailand), and [Hospitals in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospitals_in_Thailand)

A modern multi-story hospital building featuring a central clock tower with a traditional Thai roof, as seen at night.

[Siriraj Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siriraj_Hospital) in Bangkok, the oldest and largest hospital in Thailand

Thailand ranked sixth in the world and first in Asia in the 2019 [Global Health Security Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Health_Security_Index) of global health security capabilities across 195 countries,[^cite_note-330] making it the only [developing country](78449.md) in the global top ten. Thailand had 62 hospitals accredited by [Joint Commission International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Commission).[^cite_note-331] In 2002, [Bumrungrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bumrungrad_International_Hospital) became the first hospital in Asia to meet the standard.[^cite_note-332]

Health and medical care is overseen by the [Ministry of Public Health](23194319.md), with national expenditures on health amounting to 4.3 per cent of GDP in 2009. [Non-communicable diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-communicable_disease) form the major burden of morbidity and mortality, while infectious diseases including malaria[^cite_note-333] and tuberculosis,[^cite_note-334] as well as traffic accidents, are also important public health issues.[^cite_note-335]

In 2018, the interim parliament voted to legalise the use of [cannabis for medical reasons](175440.md), making Thailand the first Southeast Asian country to allow the use of medical cannabis.[^cite_note-336]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Thailand)

Young Buddhist novices in orange robes receiving incense sticks from someone outside the frame.

[Buddhist novices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Ar%C4%81ma%E1%B9%87era) receiving [joss sticks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joss_sticks).

Thai culture and traditions incorporate influences from India, China, Cambodia, and the rest of Southeast Asia. Thailand's national religion, [Theravada Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada), is central to modern [Thai identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thainess). [Thai Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Thailand) has evolved over millennia from the Mon kingdom of Dvaravati, and today incorporates regional beliefs rooted in [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), [animism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animism), and ancestor worship. The [official calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_solar_calendar) is based on the Eastern version of the [Buddhist Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_calendar) (BE).

People riding in the back of pickup trucks throwing water on a crowded street next to a moat.

The roads along the old moat of [Chiang Mai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Mai) are full of vehicles during the [Songkran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songkran_\(Thailand\)) water splashing festival.

The ancient Thai identity originates from Tai-speaking groups who migrated from southern China to Southeast Asia between the 6th and 11th centuries.[^cite_note-Stentiford-2022-337] They intermingled with existing Austroasiatic groups, such as the Mon and Khmer, absorbing their cultures and beliefs.[^cite_note-Stentiford-2022-337] King Ramkhamhaeng's reign (1279–1298) marked the inception of a unified Thai identity.[^cite_note-\:2-338] Central Thailand's prevalent culture formed the basis of the modern Thai identity, which the [Phibun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plaek_Phibunsongkhram) regime subsequently promoted nationwide during the 1940s through cultural mandates.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341]

Most of the people dressed in elaborate, colorful traditional costumes.

Poy Sang Long in [Mae Hong Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mae_Hong_Son), northern Thailand

[Overseas Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Chinese) form a significant part of Thai society, particularly in and around Bangkok, with Thai Chinese businesses prospering as part of the larger [bamboo network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamboo_network).[^cite_note-342]

A Thai man in formal attire is greeting someone with a traditional Wai gesture, hands pressed together, smiling gently, and people are smiling.

[Thai greeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_greeting), the smile is an important symbol of refinement in Thai culture.

A person holding a glowing, decorated floating lantern over a body of water at night.

People floating krathong rafts during the [Loi Krathong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loi_Krathong) festival in Chiang Mai, Thailand

Respect for the elderly and superiors is a fundamental Thai [custom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mores), reflected in the Thai language's complex [classes of honorifics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_honorifics).[^cite_note-Kummer-343] The *[wai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wai_\(gesture\))* is a standard greeting generally offered first by a person who is younger or lower in social status.[^cite_note-Smutkupt-1976-344] Older siblings also have defined duties toward younger ones.[^cite_note-345] Social [taboos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taboo) include touching someone's head or pointing with the feet, as the head is considered the most sacred part of the body and the foot the lowest.[^cite_note-Smutkupt-1976-344]

### Art

Main article: [Thai art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_art)

Traditional Thai mural painting featuring ornate, stylized figures in a chariot battle scene.

Scene from the [Ramakien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramakien) depicted on a mural at [Wat Phra Kaew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat_Phra_Kaew)

Thai art heavily relies on [Buddhist art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_art) and Indian epics, with traditional sculpture almost exclusively depicting the Buddha. Traditional paintings primarily function as book illustrations or ornamentation for palaces and temples. Originating from indigenous Mon and Khmer influences, Thai art developed its own unique style by the Sukhothai and Ayutthaya periods, later integrating [Sri Lankan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_art) and [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_art) elements.[^cite_note-Buddhist_Arts_of_Thailand-346]

Two Thai women wearing traditional silk sabai garments standing beside a wooden silk loom.

Thai women wearing [sabai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabai), [Jim Thompson House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Thompson_House)

Traditional Thai painting lacks linear [perspective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perspective_\(graphical\)), instead sizing elements based on their degree of importance. Composition relied on apportioning areas with "space transformers" to isolate main elements. Perspective was only introduced in the mid-19th century through [Western](21208200.md) influence, spearheaded by the monk-artist [Khrua In Khong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khrua_In_Khong).[^cite_note-347] Popular narrative subjects include [Jataka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jataka) stories, the Buddha's life, Buddhist cosmology, Thai versions of the *[Ramayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramayana)* and *[Mahabharata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahabharata)*, and [Thai folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_folklore).[^cite_note-Buddhist_Arts_of_Thailand-346]

### Architecture

Main article: [Architecture of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Thailand)

Two giant, colorful, ornate demon guardian statues standing on either side of a temple gate.

Two sculptures guarding the eastern gate of the main chapel at [Wat Arun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat_Arun)

[Buddhist temples in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Buddhist_temples_in_Thailand) are known as "[wats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat)", derived from the [Pāḷi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81%E1%B8%B7i) word *vāṭa* (enclosure), referencing the walls that separate the sacred space from the secular world. While *wats* display diverse regional layouts, they generally adhere to the same structural principles.[^cite_note-348] Ayutthaya Kingdom architecture, designed to display might and riches, notably featured temples with steep roofs and elaborate eaves stretching from the masterhead.[^cite_note-349]

### Literature

Main articles: [Thai literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_literature) and [Thai poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_poetry)

Statues of a male figure playing a flute and a mermaid sitting on rocks at a beach during sunrise.

Sculptures of [Phra Aphai Mani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phra_Aphai_Mani) and the Mermaid from the [epic poem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_poem) *[Phra Aphai Mani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phra_Aphai_Mani)*, a work of [Sunthorn Phu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunthorn_Phu)

Early Thai literature from the Sukhothai period was written in simple prose with basic alliteration. The [Ram Khamhaeng Inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram_Khamhaeng_Inscription) is often considered the first Thai literary work, though its authenticity remains debated.[^cite_note-350] King [Maha Thammaracha I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maha_Thammaracha_I)'s *Trai Phum [Phra Ruang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phra_Ruang)* (1345) expounds on Buddhist philosophy and is considered one of the nation's earliest theological treatises.[^cite_note-351]

The Ayutthaya period introduced new poetic forms, metres, and rhyme schemes, often combining them within a single work. Notable pieces include the narrative poem *Lilit [Yuan Phai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuan_Phai)* and Prince [Thammathibet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thammathibet)'s *Kap He Ruea*, sung during [royal barge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_barge) processions.[^cite_note-352] The Thonburi period produced the *[Ramakien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramakien)*, a verse drama contributed to by King [Taksin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taksin).

Rattanakosin-era literature often dealt with war and military strategy, reflecting ongoing conflicts with Burma. This era is heavily defined by the poet [Sunthorn Phu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunthorn_Phu), the "bard of Rattanakosin," whose masterpiece *[Phra Aphai Mani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phra_Aphai_Mani)* popularised the fantasy-adventure epic in Thai literature.[^cite_note-Embassy-353] Modern literary figures include [Kukrit Pramoj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kukrit_Pramoj), [Kulap Saipradit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kulap_Saipradit), [Chart Korbjitti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chart_Korbjitti), and [Duanwad Pimwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duanwad_Pimwana).[^cite_note-354]

### Music and dance

Main articles: [Music of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Thailand) and [Dance in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_in_Thailand)

Performers in elaborate traditional Thai costumes and gilded masks performing a classical dance.

[Khon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khon) show

Alongside folk dances like the southern [Menora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menora_\(dance\)) and central [Ramwong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romvong), Thailand's two major forms of classical dance drama are [Khon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khon) and [Lakhon nai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhon_nai). Originally exclusive to the royal courts, they eventually influenced [likay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Likay), a popular dance theatre style for commoners.[^cite_note-355] Ritual dances such as the [ram muay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ram_muay) and [wai khru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wai_khru_ram_muay) are traditionally performed before [muay Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muay_Thai) matches as an homage to teachers.[^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357] Classical Thai music is primarily performed by three ensemble types: the [Piphat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piphat), [Khrueang sai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khrueang_sai), and [Mahori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahori).[^cite_note-358]

### Media

Main articles: [Mass media in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media_in_Thailand), [Censorship in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_Thailand), and [Thai television soap opera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_television_soap_opera)

Portrait of Tony Jaa

Portrait of Lalisa Manobal

[Tony Jaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Jaa) and [Lalisa Manobal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lalisa_Manobal)

[\[viii\]](#cite_note-361)

[Thai films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Thailand) and television dramas ([Lakorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_television_soap_opera)) have developed a strong regional and international presence.[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363] Notable cinematic exports include the critically acclaimed horror film *[Shutter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shutter_\(2004_film\))* (2004), the martial arts hits *[Ong-Bak: Muay Thai Warrior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ong-Bak:_Muay_Thai_Warrior)* (2003) and *[Tom-Yum-Goong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom-Yum-Goong)* (2005) starring [Tony Jaa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Jaa), and the thriller *[Bad Genius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bad_Genius_\(2017_film\))* (2017), which broke box office records across Asia.[^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365] Economically, the entertainment industry is a major contributor to Thailand's GDP, generating billions in direct revenue and supporting tens of thousands of jobs.[^cite_note-366] Thailand also produces internationally successful [dance-pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance-pop) artists like [Lisa](59226766.md),[^cite_note-367] [Violette Wautier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violette_Wautier),[^cite_note-368] [Jeff Satur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Satur),[^cite_note-369] and [Tata Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tata_Young).

### Cuisine

Main article: [Thai cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_cuisine)

Bowl of chicken massaman curry with potatoes and peanuts

Plate of sliced ripe yellow mango next to sticky rice topped with coconut milk

Bowl of spicy, orange-colored tom yum soup with shrimp

Plate of pad Thai noodles with shrimp and garnishes

**Clockwise from top left**: [Chicken massaman curry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massaman_curry); [mango sticky rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mango_sticky_rice); [pad Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pad_Thai); and [tom yum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_yum)

Thai cuisine is highly celebrated globally, characterised by ingredients like [garlic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garlic), [lemongrass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lemongrass), [kaffir lime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaffir_lime), [galangal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galangal), [turmeric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turmeric), [coriander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coriander), and [coconut milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut_milk).[^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] Cuisine varies distinctly by region: *kaeng khiao wan* ([green curry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_curry)) in the central region, *som tam* ([green papaya salad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_papaya_salad)) in the northeast, *[khao soi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khao_soi)* in the north, and [*massaman* curry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massaman_curry) in the south.[^cite_note-372] In 2017, seven Thai dishes—including *[tom yam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_yum) goong*, *[pad thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pad_thai)*, and *massaman* curry—were featured on [CNN Travel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN_Travel)'s "World's 50 Best Foods" list, more than from any other country.[^cite_note-373]

Rice, particularly [jasmine rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasmine_rice), is the staple of almost every meal. Thailand is a top global exporter of rice, with domestic consumption exceeding 100 kg per person annually.[^cite_note-374] The country is also renowned for its vast [street food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_food_of_Thailand) culture—with Bangkok often called the street food capital of the world—and is a global leader in the [edible insect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insects_as_food) industry.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]

### Units of measurement

Further information: [Thai units of measurement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_units_of_measurement)

Thailand predominantly uses the [metric system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metric_system). However, [traditional units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_units_of_measurement) are still used for land area, and [imperial units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_units) are occasionally used for building materials. The [Buddhist Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_calendar) (B.E.) year is standard in government, education, and the civil service, while the [Common Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era) is increasingly used in banking, industry, and commerce.[^cite_note-377]

### Sport

Main article: [Sport in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Thailand)

See also: [Thailand at the Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thailand_at_the_Olympics), [Rugby union in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_union_in_Thailand), [Golf in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golf_in_Thailand), [Football in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Thailand), and [List of sporting events held in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sporting_events_held_in_Thailand)

Two Muay Thai fighters in a boxing ring engaged in close combat, with one fighter throwing a knee strike.

[Muay Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muay_Thai), Thailand's signature sport

[Muay Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muay_Thai) (Thai boxing) is a native [combat sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combat_sport) famed for its stand-up striking and [clinching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinch_fighting) techniques. It gained international prominence in the late 20th century through practitioners like [Buakaw Banchamek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buakaw_Banchamek) and [Samart Payakaroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samart_Payakaroon).[^cite_note-378] Major fights take place at venues like the renowned [Lumpinee Boxing Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumpinee_Boxing_Stadium) in Bangkok. Today, [Association football](10568.md) has overtaken Muay Thai as the most widely followed sport in the country. The [Thailand national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thailand_national_football_team) has competed in the [AFC Asian Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFC_Asian_Cup) six times, and Thailand co-hosted the tournament in 1972 and 2007.[^cite_note-379]

[Volleyball](32558.md) is rapidly growing in popularity, led by the highly successful [women's national team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thailand_women's_national_volleyball_team), which has won the [Asian Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Women's_Volleyball_Championship) multiple times. Another traditional sport is [Sepak takraw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sepak_takraw), a native game similar to volleyball where players use only their feet, knees, chest, and head to hit a rattan ball. Thailand also features over 200 world-class golf courses, attracting golf tourism from across Asia and Western countries.[^cite_note-380] The country's largest sporting arena is [Rajamangala National Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajamangala_National_Stadium), with a capacity of approximately 50,000.[^cite_note-381]
